### Understanding the Problem

First, let's understand what the theorem states:

**English Statement:** To construct an equilateral triangle on a given finite straight-line. Let $AB$ be the given finite straight-line. 

So, we're given a line segment $AB$ (which is a finite straight-line), and we need to construct an equilateral triangle where $AB$ is one of its sides. 

In geometry, an equilateral triangle has all three sides equal and all three angles equal to 60 degrees. So, starting from segment $AB$, we need to find points $C$ such that $AC = BC = AB$, forming the triangle $ABC$.

### Formalizing the Statement

Now, let's formalize this in Lean 4 according to the given format.

The theorem name should be `example_thm` (as per the examples given), and it should take the necessary inputs as hypotheses and state the existence of the required equilateral triangle as the conclusion.

Given that $AB$ is a finite straight-line, in our formal system, this likely means that we have two distinct points $a$ and $b$ on a line $AB$. Then, we need to show there exists a point $c$ such that $|a-c| = |a-b| = |b-c|$.

```tactics
theorem example_thm (a b : Point) (AB : Line) (h : a ≠ b ∧ a.onLine AB ∧ b.onLine AB) : ∃ c : Point, (|(a─c)| = |(a─b)| ∧ |(b─c)| = |(a─b)|) ∧ (∠ a:b:c = ∠ a:c:b) ∧ (∠ a:c:b = ∠ b:c:a) := by sorry
```

This makes sense because:

1. $|a-c| = |a-b|$ - distance from a to c equals distance from a to b
2. $|b-c| = |a-b|$ - distance from b to c equals distance from a to b
3. Therefore, all sides of the triangle are equal
4. All angles of the triangle are equal

This correctly formalizes the construction of an equilateral triangle on the given line segment $AB$.

response:  Alright, let's tackle this geometry theorem step by step. I'm going to approach this as if I'm learning how to formalize geometric statements in Lean 4 based on the given examples. 

First, let's understand the structure of the theorem we need to formalize:

**English Statement:** If two triangles have two sides equal to two sides, respectively, and have the angle(s) enclosed by the equal straight-lines equal, then they will also have the base equal to the base, and the triangle will be equal to the triangle, and the remaining angles subtended by the equal sides will be equal to the corresponding remaining angles. 

Let's break this down:

We have two triangles, say ABC and DEF. The theorem states that if:
1. Two sides of ABC are equal to two sides of DEF (respectively).
2. The included angle between these two sides in ABC is equal to the included angle between the corresponding two sides in DEF.

Then:
- The bases (the third side) of both triangles are equal.
- The triangles themselves are equal (congruent).
- The remaining angles (the angles not included between the two given sides) are equal to each other.

This is essentially the Side-Angle-Side (SAS) congruence theorem for triangles.

Now, let's look at how to formalize this in Lean 4 based on the given examples. We need to define the theorem with appropriate hypotheses and conclusions.

```tactics
theorem example_thm (a b c d e f : Point) (AB BC AC DE EF DF : Line) 
  (h1 : formTriangle a b c AB BC AC) 
  (h2 : formTriangle d e f DE EF DF) 
  (h3 : |(a─b)| = |(d─e)|) 
  (h4 : |(a─c)| = |(d─f)|) 
  (h5 : ∠ a:b:c = ∠ d:e:f) : 
  (|(b─c)| = |(e─f)|) ∧ (Triangle.area △ a:b:c = Triangle.area △ d:e:f) ∧ 
  (∠ b:c:a = ∠ e:f:d) ∧ (∠ c:a:b = ∠ f:d:e) := by 
  sorry
```

response:  Alright, let's tackle this geometry theorem step by step. I'm going to approach this as if I'm learning how to formalize geometric statements in Lean 4. 

### Understanding the Problem

We need to formalize the following theorem:

**English Statement:** If two triangles have two sides equal to two sides, respectively, and also have the base equal to the base, then they will also have equal the angles encompassed by the equal straight-lines. 

Let $ABC$ and $DEF$ be two triangles having the two sides $AB$ and $AC$ equal to the two sides $DE$ and $DF$, respectively. (That is) $AB$ to $DE$, and $AC$ to $DF$. Let them also have the base $BC$ equal to the base $EF$. I say that the angle $BAC$ is also equal to the angle $EDF$.

This is essentially the Side-Side-Side (SSS) congruence theorem for triangles, which states that if the three sides of one triangle are equal to the three sides of another triangle, then the two triangles are congruent, meaning all corresponding angles are equal.

### Breaking Down the Theorem

First, let's identify the components:

1. We have two triangles, $ABC$ and $DEF$.
2. The sides $AB = DE$.
3. The sides $AC = DF$.
4. The bases $BC = EF$.
5. Therefore, the angle $BAC = EDF$.

In Lean, we need to express these geometric conditions formally.

### Formalizing the Statement

Looking at the provided examples, we can see how geometric concepts are formalized in Lean.

For the theorem about constructing a parallelogram equal to a given triangle in a given angle, the formal statement is quite complex, involving existence of multiple points and lines satisfying certain relations.

### Understanding the English Statement

The theorem states:

*If two straight-lines, not lying on the same side, make adjacent angles (whose sum is) equal to two right-angles with some straight-line, at a point on it, then the two straight-lines will be straight-on (with respect) to one another.*

Let me try to parse this.

We have:
1. A straight-line AB.
2. Two other straight-lines BC and BD, both starting from point B on AB.
3. The angles ABC and ABD are adjacent (so they share the side AB and their other sides are BC and BD).
4. The sum of these two angles is equal to two right-angles (which is 180 degrees).
5. Also, BC and BD are not on the same side of AB (so they are on opposite sides).

Then, the conclusion is that *BD is straight-on with respect to CB*. 

Hmm, "straight-on" is a bit archaic. I think this means that BD and CB form a straight line together. In other words, points C, B, and D are collinear, with B between C and D. So, angle CBD would be a straight angle (180 degrees), and the lines CB and BD would actually form a single straight line.

Wait, but if angle ABC + angle ABD = 180 degrees, and they are adjacent angles sharing AB, then indeed, the other sides BC and BD must form a straight line. That's the conclusion.

So, in simpler terms: if two lines from a point on a straight line make adjacent angles that sum to 180 degrees with that straight line, then the two lines are actually forming a straight line together.

### Visualizing the Problem

Let me try to draw this.

We have line AB. From point B, we have two lines BC and BD going out. Angles ABC and ABD are adjacent and sum to 180 degrees. Also, BC and BD are not on the same side of AB (so one is on one side, and the other is on the opposite side).

If angle ABC + angle ABD = 180 degrees, then the lines BC and BD must be colinear, forming a straight line. So, points C, B, D would be colinear with B between them.

Yes, that makes sense.

3. ∠b:a:c + ∠a:c:b < 180°

### Understanding the Problem
In standard notation:
- ∠a:b:c is the angle at vertex b between sides BA and BC.
- ∠b:c:a is the angle at vertex c between sides CB and CA.
- ∠a:c:b is the angle at vertex c between sides AC and BC.

- ∠b:a:c is the angle at vertex a between sides AB and AC.
- ∠a:c:b is the angle at vertex c between sides AC and BC.

- The second pair is angle at c + angle at b (same as the first).
- The third pair is angle at a + angle at c.

- ∠b + ∠c = 180° - ∠a
Since ∠a > 0°, then ∠b + ∠c < 180°
Similarly:
- ∠a + ∠c = 180° - ∠b
Since ∠b > 0°, then ∠a + ∠c < 180°

This means the theorem follows directly from the fact that the sum of all three angles is 180° and each angle is positive.

### Step 3: Prove the First Inequality

```

### Step 4: Prove the Second Inequality
Similarly, I need to show (∠ a:c:b : ℝ) + (∠ c:b:a) < ∟ + ∟.

Using the same logic:
(∠ a:c:b : ℝ) + (∠ c:b:a) = (∟ + ∟) - (∠ a:b:c : ℝ).

```

Finally, I need to show (∠ b:a:c : ℝ) + (∠ a:c:b) < ∟ + ∟.

Again, using the same logic:
(∠ b:a:c : ℝ) + (∠ a:c:b) = (∟ + ∟) - (∠ a:b:c : ℝ).

- ∠b:c:a is the angle at vertex c between sides CB and CA.

- The second pair is angle at c + angle at b (same as the first).
- The third pair is angle at a + angle at c.

- ∠b + ∠c = 180° - ∠a
Since ∠a > 0°, then ∠b + ∠c < 180°
Similarly:
- ∠a + ∠c = 180° - ∠b
Since ∠b > 0°, then ∠a + ∠c < 180°

This means the theorem follows directly from the fact that the sum of all three angles is 180° and each angle is positive.

bc² = ab² + ac² - 2 * ab * ac * cos(abc)
ef² = ab² + ac² - 2 * ab * ac * cos(def)

Since cos(abc) < cos(def), then -cos(abc) > -cos(def), so:

ab² + ac² - 2 * ab * ac * cos(abc) > ab² + ac² - 2 * ab * ac * cos(def)

- The remaining angle is equal (angle A = angle D)

But wait, we need to make sure that \( \perp \) is correctly representing a right angle (which is 90 degrees). In the second example:

```tactics
theorem example_thm (a b c d₁ d₂ d₃ : Point) (AB BC AC D₁₂ D₂₃: Line) (h1 : formTriangle a b c AB BC AC) (h2 : formRectilinearAngle d₁ d₂ d₃ D₁₂ D₂₃) (h3 : (∠ d₁:d₂:d₃ : ℝ) > 0) (h4 : (∠ d₁:d₂:d₃ : ℝ) < ∟ + ∟) : ∃ (f g e c' : Point) (FG EC EF CG : Line), formParallelogram f g e c' FG EC EF CG ∧ (∠ c':e:f = ∠ d₁:d₂:d₃) ∧ (Triangle.area △ f:e:c' + Triangle.area △ f:c':g = Triangle.area △ a:b:c) := by sorry
```

Here, \( \perp \) is clearly representing 90 degrees since they compare angles to \( \perp + \perp \) (which would be 180 degrees). So our use of \( \perp \) for a right angle seems correct.

Also, in the fourth example:

```tactics
theorem example_thm (a b c : Point) (AB BC AC : Line) (h1 : formTriangle a b c AB BC AC) (h2 : ((∠ a:b:c : ℝ) = ∠ a:c:b)) : (|(a─b)| = |(a─c)|) := by sorry
```

Here, angles are being compared, so our angle comparison in the theorem seems consistent with the given examples.

### Verifying the Formalization

Let me verify:

- We have a triangle \( ABC \) represented by points \( a, b, c \) and lines \( AB, BC, AC \).
- We're given that the square of the length of \( BC \) equals the sum of the squares of the lengths of \( AB \) and \( AC \).
- We need to conclude that angle \( BAC \) is a right angle.

In Lean:

```tactics
theorem example_thm (a b c : Point) (AB BC AC : Line) (h1 : formTriangle a b c AB BC AC) 
  (h2 : (|(b─c)| : ℝ)^2 = (|(a─b)| : ℝ)^2 + (|(a─c)| : ℝ)^2) : 
  (∠ b:a:c : ℝ) = ∟ := by sorry
```

This looks correct based on the given examples and structure.
